<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Electrolyte Imbalance & Membrane Excitability
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Severe hyperkalemia causes sustained membrane depolarization, leading to inactivation of voltage-gated sodium channels, resulting in loss of membrane excitability and conduction failure in both cardiac and skeletal muscle tissue. (严重高钾血症导致持续的膜去极化，从而使电压门控钠通道失活，导致心肌和骨骼肌兴奋性丧失和传导阻滞。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 60-year-old male with a history of end-stage renal disease (ESRD), type 2 diabetes mellitus, and coronary artery disease presents to the emergency department after missing three consecutive hemodialysis sessions. He is profoundly lethargic and exhibits generalized flaccid muscle weakness and hyporeflexia. Vital signs show a heart rate of 55 bpm, blood pressure 90/60 mmHg, and respiratory rate 14 breaths/min. Laboratory results reveal: serum sodium 134 mEq/L, potassium 8.6 mEq/L, calcium (total) 6.2 mg/dL (ionized Ca2+ 0.72 mmol/L), bicarbonate 11 mEq/L, and pH 7.18. A 12-lead ECG shows a widened QRS complex merging with the T wave, creating a sinusoidal (sine-wave) pattern. Which of the following physiological mechanisms best explains both the patient's flaccid paralysis and the ECG findings?

(A) Increased extracellular potassium concentration shifts the resting membrane potential towards the equilibrium potential for potassium, leading to increased influx of calcium ions through voltage-gated calcium channels, causing sustained muscle contraction and tetany.
(B) Metabolic acidosis causes a shift of potassium ions from the intracellular to the extracellular space, increasing the driving force for potassium efflux through potassium leak channels, leading to hyperpolarization of the resting membrane potential and decreased excitability.
(C) Hypocalcemia increases the binding affinity of sodium channels to intracellular sodium, leading to a more negative resting membrane potential and reduced sodium influx during depolarization, resulting in slowed conduction velocity.
(D) Severe hyperkalemia causes sustained depolarization of the resting membrane potential, leading to inactivation of voltage-gated sodium channels and rendering the cell membrane refractory to action potential generation.
(E) The combination of hyperkalemia and metabolic acidosis increases the concentration gradient for chloride ions, leading to increased chloride influx through voltage-gated chloride channels, causing hyperpolarization and inhibition of neurotransmitter release.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 60-year-old male with ESRD, T2DM, CAD, missed dialysis. This points towards potential electrolyte imbalances, particularly hyperkalemia, due to impaired renal excretion.
- **Symptoms:** Lethargy, flaccid muscle weakness, hyporeflexia. These suggest impaired neuromuscular excitability, potentially due to membrane depolarization or hyperpolarization.
- **Labs:**
    - K+ 8.6 mEq/L (High): Severe hyperkalemia is confirmed.
    - Ca2+ 6.2 mg/dL (Low), Ionized Ca2+ 0.72 mmol/L (Low): Hypocalcemia is present.
    - pH 7.18 (Low), HCO3- 11 mEq/L (Low): Metabolic acidosis is present.
- **ECG:** Widened QRS merging with T wave (sine wave). This is a classic sign of severe hyperkalemia, indicating slowed and abnormal cardiac conduction.
- **Question:** Asks for the fundamental biophysical mechanism explaining both flaccid paralysis and the ECG findings in the context of these electrolyte abnormalities.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Hyperkalemia and Resting Membrane Potential (RMP):** The normal RMP is primarily determined by potassium efflux through K+ leak channels and is close to the Nernst potential for K+ (EK ≈ -95 mV). The Nernst equation for K+ is EK = (RT/zF) * ln([K+]out/[K+]in). With severe hyperkalemia ([K+]out = 8.6 mEq/L), the extracellular potassium concentration is significantly elevated. This reduces the electrochemical gradient for K+ efflux, causing less K+ to leave the cell. Consequently, the RMP becomes less negative (depolarized), moving closer to EK. Using the Nernst equation: EK = (61.5 mV / 1) * log10([4.0 mEq/L] / [8.6 mEq/L]) ≈ -72 mV.
- **Voltage-Gated Sodium Channels (Nav):** These channels are crucial for the rapid depolarization phase (upstroke) of action potentials in excitable cells (neurons, muscle cells, cardiac myocytes). They exist in three main states: closed (resting), open (activated), and inactivated. The transition from closed to open requires depolarization to the threshold potential. The transition from open to inactivated occurs during sustained depolarization. Inactivation involves a conformational change (the "inactivation gate" or "h-gate" closes) that blocks ion flow even if the membrane remains depolarized.
- **Mechanism of Paralysis and Conduction Failure:** The sustained depolarization caused by hyperkalemia (RMP moving towards -72 mV) keeps the voltage-gated sodium channels in the inactivated state for a prolonged period. Because the membrane potential is not sufficiently negative to allow the channels to recover from inactivation (i.e., return to the closed state), they cannot open in response to a stimulus. This prevents the generation of action potentials, leading to loss of excitability and conduction failure. In skeletal muscle, this manifests as flaccid paralysis and hyporeflexia. In cardiac myocytes, it leads to slowed conduction (widened QRS), impaired repolarization (peaked T waves initially, then merging QRS-T), and ultimately, ventricular fibrillation or asystole (sine wave pattern represents near-complete standstill or very slow, disorganized activity).
- **Role of Hypocalcemia:** Low ionized calcium (Ca2+) reduces the electrostatic attraction between the negative resting membrane potential and the positive charges on the extracellular surface of the sodium channel protein. This effectively raises the voltage threshold required to open the Nav channels, further impairing excitability and exacerbating the effects of hyperkalemia.
- **Role of Acidosis:** Metabolic acidosis (low pH) promotes the movement of H+ ions into cells in exchange for K+ ions moving out, further contributing to extracellular hyperkalemia.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. Hyperkalemia causes depolarization, not increased calcium influx leading to contraction. Increased extracellular K+ *reduces* the driving force for Ca2+ influx and *stabilizes* the membrane, inhibiting contraction. Hypocalcemia also impairs muscle contraction, but the primary mechanism for paralysis and ECG changes in severe hyperkalemia is channel inactivation due to depolarization. Tetany is associated with hypocalcemia and hypomagnesemia, but not typically the primary presentation of severe hyperkalemia.
- (B): Incorrect. While acidosis does shift K+ out of cells, worsening hyperkalemia, the primary mechanism causing paralysis and ECG changes is the *depolarization* effect of hyperkalemia, not hyperpolarization. Hyperpolarization would *decrease* excitability, but the depolarization caused by hyperkalemia is the key driver of Nav channel inactivation. Also, K+ efflux is primarily through leak channels, not driven by a large electrochemical gradient in this scenario.
- (C): Incorrect. Hypocalcemia *increases* the voltage threshold for Nav channel activation, making depolarization *harder*, not easier. It exacerbates the inexcitability caused by hyperkalemia but is not the fundamental mechanism itself. The fundamental mechanism is the inactivation of already present Nav channels due to sustained depolarization.
- (E): Incorrect. While chloride channels exist, their primary role in this context is not hyperpolarization. The main ionic events are related to potassium and sodium channel function and the resting membrane potential shift. Acidosis can affect chloride transport, but it doesn't explain the core mechanism of paralysis and conduction failure due to hyperkalemia.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Severe hyperkalemia causes sustained membrane depolarization, leading to inactivation of voltage-gated sodium channels and loss of excitability. This explains both flaccid paralysis (skeletal muscle) and conduction abnormalities (cardiac muscle). Key differentials include hypocalcemia (which exacerbates hyperkalemia's effects) and metabolic acidosis (which worsens hyperkalemia). Remember the classic ECG progression in hyperkalemia: peaked T waves -> PR prolongation -> QRS widening -> loss of P waves -> sine wave -> asystole/VF.
